Zeta elements for elliptic curves and applications
Ashay A. Burungale, Christopher Skinner, Ye Tian and Xin Wan

Abstract
. Let E E be an elliptic curve defined over ℚ {\mathbb{Q}} with conductor N N and p ∤ 2 ​ N p\nmid 2N a prime. Let L L be an imaginary quadratic field with p p split. We prove the existence of p p -adic zeta element for E E over L L , encoding two different p p -adic L L -functions associated to E E over L L via explicit reciprocity laws at the primes above p p . We formulate a main conjecture for E E over L L in terms of the zeta element, mediating different main conjectures in which the p p -adic L L -functions appear, and prove some results toward them. The zeta element has various applications to the arithmetic of elliptic curves. This includes a proof of main conjecture for semistable elliptic curves E E over ℚ {\mathbb{Q}} at supersingular primes p p , as conjectured by Kobayashi in 2002. It leads to the p p -part of the conjectural Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer (BSD) formula for such curves of analytic rank zero or one, and enables us to present the first infinite families of non-CM elliptic curves for which the BSD conjecture is true. We provide further evidence towards the BSD conjecture: new cases of p p -converse to the Gross–Zagier and Kolyvagin theorem, and p p -part of the BSD formula for ordinary primes p p . Along the way, we give a proof of a conjecture of Perrin-Riou connecting Beilinson–Kato elements with rational points.



1.
Introduction

A principle of Kato posits the existence of
p
-adic zeta element for a motive over a number field: an arithmetic incarnation of the associated critical
L
-values in
p
-adic étale cohomology. Cyclotomic units, elliptic units and Beilinson–Kato elements are primary known examples of zeta elements, the latter for an elliptic curve
E
defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
The aim of this paper is prove the existence of a
p
-adic zeta element for such an
E
together with an imaginary quadratic field
L
(cf. Theorem
1.14
).

The zeta element is ancillary to the Iwasawa theory of
E
over
L
, and in turn to the arithmetic of
E
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. It leads to a proof of the
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
-Iwasawa main conjecture at supersingular primes, complementing the work of Kato
[
79
]
and Skinner and Urban
[
131
]
at ordinary primes:
we establish Kobayashi’s main Conjecture for semistable
E
at supersingular primes
p
(cf. Theorem
1.3
).
In combination with the
p
-adic Gross–Zagier formula, in the supersingular case the main conjecture yields the
p
-part of the conjectural Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer formula if the analytic rank of
E
is zero or one (cf. Theorem
1.5
), as well as a
p
-converse to the theorem of Gross–Zagier and Kolyvagin for
E
(cf. Theorem
1.6
).
In turn this leads to the first infinite families of non-CM elliptic curves for which the full BSD formula is proved to hold (cf. Theorem
1.7
).

Exploiting the zeta element, we also provide additional evidence towards the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture,
such as new cases of the
p
-part of the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer formula (cf. Theorem
1.9
) and
p
-converse for ordinary primes
p
(cf. Theorem
1.10
).
Another application concerns a conjecture of Perrin-Riou (cf. Theorem
1.13
).

While the main text considers weight two elliptic newforms, in this introduction we focus on the case of elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

1.1.
Arithmetic of elliptic curves

We first describe some of the applications of the zeta element to the arithmetic of elliptic curves.

The Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture

Let
E
be an elliptic curve defined over the rationals.
A fundamental arithmetic invariant of
E
is its Mordell–Weil rank, that is the rank of the finitely generated abelian group

E({\mathbb{Q}}).

As
E
varies, this rank is typically expected to be
0
or
1
but is difficult to get a handle on generally. Another mysterious structure in the arithmetic of
E
is the conjecturally finite Tate–Shafarevich group
\hbox{\cyr X}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
. For a prime
p
, the
p^{\infty}
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{p^{\infty}}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
links these two
via the fundamental exact sequence

0\rightarrow E({\mathbb{Q}})\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Sel}}_{p^{\infty}}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})\rightarrow\hbox{\cyr X}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})[p^{\infty}]\rightarrow 0.

(1.1)

On the analytic side, the primary object of interest is the complex
L
-function
L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
,
with
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and the fundamental analytic invariant is the analytic rank, defined as the vanishing order
{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=1}L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
.

The Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture (BSD) conjecture predicts a mysterious link between the arithmetic and analytic invariants:

Conjecture 1.1

(The Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture for ranks
0
and
1
)
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve defined over the rationals. For
r\in\{0,1\}
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

{\mathrm{rank}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}E({\mathbb{Q}})=r
and
\hbox{\cyr X}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
is finite.

(2)

{\mathrm{corank}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathrm{Sel}}_{p^{\infty}}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=r
for a prime
p
.

(3)

{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=1}L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=r
.

Moreover, for a prime
p
the
p
-part of the BSD formula holds under any of the above,
that is,

\bigg|\frac{L^{(r)}(1,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})}{\Omega_{E}R(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})}\bigg|_{p}^{-1}=\bigg|\frac{\#\hbox{\cyr X}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})\cdot\prod_{q|N}c_{q}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})}{\#E({\mathbb{Q}})^{2}}\bigg|_{p}^{-1}

for
\Omega_{E}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
the Néron period,
R(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
the regulator of
E({\mathbb{Q}})
,
N
the conductor of
E
,

c_{q}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
the Tamagawa number at a prime
q
, and
|\cdot|_{p}
the
p
-adic absolute value normalised so that
|p|_{p}=\frac{1}{p}
.

Note that (1) implies (2) by the exact sequence (
1.1
). That (3) implies (1) is a fundamental result towards the BSD conjecture due to
Gross–Zagier, Kolyvagin and Rubin in the late 80’s. Beginning with the work of Skinner and Zhang a decade back, the implication (2) implies (3) is referred to as a
p
-converse to the Gross–Zagier and Kolyvagin theorem: a
p
-adic criterion for
E
to have analytic rank
r
. In this paper we prove new cases of the
p
-converse and the
p
-part of the BSD formula for both ordinary and supersingular primes, with an emphasis on the latter.

1.1.1.
Kobayashi’s supersingular main conjecture

Mazur
[
102
]
initiated the
Iwasawa theory of elliptic curves in the early 70’s, formulating a main conjecture for primes
p
of ordinary reduction. The case of primes of supersingular reduction exhibits new phenomena and a conjectural framework reflecting them remained elusive until the early 2000’s, when, sparked by the work of Pollack
[
114
]
, Kobayashi
[
87
]
formulated a signed Iwasawa main conjecture for elliptic curves at supersingular primes.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
, and
p\nmid 2N
a supersingular prime satisfying
(
1.7
)
(this is only an extra condition when
p=3
).
Let
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\infty}
be the cyclotomic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\Gamma={\mathrm{Gal}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{\infty}/{\mathbb{Q}})
and
\gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}
a topological generator of
\Gamma
. Put
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\![\Gamma]\!]
, viewed as a
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-module via the canonical projection
\Psi:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma
. Let
\epsilon
denote the
p
-adic cyclotomic character
\epsilon:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
.

For
\zeta
a primitive
p^{t}
-th root of unity, let

\psi_{\zeta}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma\rightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{\times}

be the finite order character induced by
\gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}\mapsto\zeta
.
For
t>0
, let
\psi_{\zeta}
also denote the Dirichlet character of
({\mathbb{Z}}/p^{t+1}{\mathbb{Z}})^{\times}
of
p
-power order such that the image of
\epsilon(\gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}})\in 1+p{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
maps to
\zeta
.
Let

\phi_{\zeta}:\Lambda\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\zeta]\subset\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}

be the homomorphism such that
\gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}\mapsto\zeta
.

Let
T
denote the
p
-adic Tate module of
E
. Let

M=T(1)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\Lambda^{\vee}

be a discrete
\Lambda
-module with the
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
-action on
\Lambda^{\vee}
via
\Psi^{-1}
. For
\circ\in\{+,-\}
, let

H^{1}_{\circ}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},M)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},M)

be the annihilator of Kobayashi’s signed submodule

H^{1}_{\circ}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T(1)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\Lambda)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T(1)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\Lambda)
under the Pontryagin duality pairing (cf. §
3.1
).
Following Kobayashi,
for
\Sigma
a finite set of places of
{\mathbb{Q}}
containing those dividing
N\infty
, define

S_{\circ}(E)=\ker\big\{H^{1}(G_{\Sigma},M)\rightarrow\prod_{v\in\Sigma,v\nmid p}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{v},M)\times\frac{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},M)}{H^{1}_{\circ}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},M)}\big\}

(1.2)

and let
X_{\circ}(E)
denote its Pontryagin dual. Based on the work of Kato
[
79
]
, Kobayashi
[
87
]
proved that
X_{\circ}(E)
is a torsion
\Lambda
-module.

Also for
p\nmid 2N
a supersingular prime satisfying (
1.7
),
Pollack
[
114
]
proved the existence of
p
-adic
L
-functions

\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\circ}(E)\in\Lambda\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\![X]\!]
,
\circ\in\{\pm\}
,
such that for
\zeta
as above with
t=0
or
t>0
and even if
\circ=+
and
t=0
or
t>0
and odd if
\circ=-
,

\phi_{\zeta}(\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\circ}(E))=e_{p}^{\circ}(\zeta)\cdot\frac{L(1,E\otimes\psi_{\zeta}^{-1})}{\Omega_{E}}

with

e_{p}^{+}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\frac{t+2}{2}}\cdot\frac{p^{t+1}}{\mathfrak{g}(\psi_{\zeta}^{-1})}\cdot\prod_{\text{odd }m=1}^{t-1}\Phi_{p^{m}}(\zeta)^{-1}&if $t>0$ even\\
2&if $t=0$.\end{cases}

and

e_{p}^{-}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\frac{t+1}{2}}\cdot\frac{p^{t+1}}{\mathfrak{g}(\psi_{\zeta}^{-1})}\cdot\prod_{\text{even }m=2}^{t-1}\Phi_{p^{m}}(\zeta)^{-1}&if $t>0$ odd\\
p-1&if $t=0$.\end{cases}

Here
\mathfrak{g}(\psi_{\zeta}^{-1})
denotes the Gauss sum and
\Phi_{p^{m}}(X)
the
p^{m}
-th cyclotomic polynomial.

Kobayashi
[
87
]
proposed the following signed Main Conjecture:

Conjecture 1.2
.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
, and
p\nmid 2N
a supersingular prime. If
p=3
, suppose that
(
1.7
) holds. For
\circ\in\{+,-\}
, we have

({\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\circ}(E))=\xi_{\Lambda}(X_{\circ}(E))

in
\Lambda
, where
\xi_{\Lambda}(\cdot)
denotes the
\Lambda
-characteristic ideal.

Our main result towards Kobayashi’s conjecture is the following:

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a semistable elliptic curve, and
p>2
a supersingular prime. If
p=3
, suppose that
(
1.7
) holds. Then Kobayashi’s Conjecture
1.2
is true, i.e.
for
\circ\in\{+,-\}
, we have

({\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\circ}(E))=\xi_{\Lambda}(X_{\circ}(E)).

Moreover, the same holds for any quadratic twist
E^{K}:=E\otimes\chi_{K}
for
\chi_{K}
the character associated to a quadratic field extension
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
with discriminant coprime to
Np

and divisible only by primes of ordinary reduction for
E
.

This provides the first cases of Kobayashi’s main conjecture for non-CM elliptic curves.

Remark 1.4
.

(i)

The above theorem was first announced by the fourth-named author in 2014 in
[
143
]
.
The pertinent parts of this paper supersede the prior announcement,
and the proof realises the strategy outlined therein. The preprint
[
143
]
is no longer intended for publication.

(ii)

The CM case of Conjecture
1.2
was established by Pollack and Rubin
[
115
]
in 2004.

Theorem
1.3
has the following applications to the BSD conjecture.

p
-part of the BSD formula

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a semistable elliptic curve, and
p>2
a supersingular prime. If
p=3
, suppose that
(
1.7
) holds.
If
{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=1}L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=r\leq 1
, then the
p
-part of the BSD formula is true, i.e.

\bigg|\frac{L^{(r)}(1,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})}{\Omega_{E}R(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})}\bigg|_{p}^{-1}=\bigg|\#\hbox{\cyr X}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})\cdot\prod_{q|N}c_{q}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})\bigg|_{p}^{-1}

for
\Omega_{E}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
the Néron period and
R(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
the regulator. Moreover, the same holds for any quadratic twist
E^{K}
as in Theorem
1.3
.

The proof of the
r=1
case is based on the
p
-adic Gross–Zagier formula
[
88
]
.

p
-converse to the Gross–Zagier and Kolyvagin theorem

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a semistable elliptic curve, and
p>2
a supersingular prime. If
p=3
, suppose that
(
1.7
) holds. Then

{\mathrm{corank}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathrm{Sel}}_{p^{\infty}}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=0\implies L(1,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})\neq 0.

Moreover, the same holds for any quadratic twist
E^{K}
as in Theorem
1.3
.

The BSD conjecture for infinite families of non-CM curves

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
denoted by

46a1
,
62a1
,
66b1
,

69a1
,
77c1
,
94a1
,
105a1
,
106d1
,

114b1
,
115a1
,
118c1
,
118d1
,
141b1
,
141c1
,
141e1

or
142c1

in Cremona’s labelling.
Let
M>1
be a square-free integer with
(M,N)=1
and
E^{M}
the quadratic twist of
E
by the character associated to the extension
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{M})/{\mathbb{Q}}
. Suppose that the following conditions hold:

(a)

L(1,E^{M})\not=0
, and

(b)

E
has ordinary reduction at the primes dividing
M
.

Then the BSD
conjecture holds for

E^{M}
, i.e.
E^{M}({\mathbb{Q}})
and
\hbox{\cyr X}(E^{M})
are finite, and

\frac{L(1,E^{M})}{\Omega_{E^{M}}}=\frac{\#\hbox{\cyr X}(E^{M})\cdot\prod_{\ell\nmid\infty}c_{\ell}(E^{M})}{\#E^{M}({\mathbb{Q}})_{{\mathrm{tor}}}^{2}}.

Moreover, the conditions (a) and (b) are satisfied by infinitely many
M
.

Our proof of this theorem is based on Theorem
1.5
and
prior work on the
p
-part of the BSD formula. The existence of infinitely many
M
satisfying the conditions (a) and (b) relies on
[
41
,
148
]
.

Remark 1.8
.

(i)

The full BSD conjecture for elliptic curves without complex multiplication had previously only been established for finitely many such curves, combining theoretical results and numerical computations.

(ii)

Theorem
1.7
yields new cases of the conjecture of Flach and Morin
[
53
]
for zeta functions of arithmetic surfaces: Let
X_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}

be a principal homogeneous space of
E^{M}_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
as in Theorem
1.7
and
{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Spec}}({\mathbb{Z}})
a proper regular model of
X
. Then
{\rm Br}({\mathcal{X}})
is finite and
the special value conjecture
[
53
, Conj. 5.12]
for
\zeta({\mathcal{X}},s)
at
s=1
holds true (cf.
[
54
]
).

Kolyvagin’s conjecture

Theorem
1.3
also has application to Kolyvagin’s conjecture on the non-triviality of the Euler system of Beilinson–Kato elements and the Heegner point Kolyvagin system,
but we do not elaborate on this here and instead refer the reader to
[
6
,
83
,
84
,
136
]
.

1.1.2.
Special cases of the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture: ordinary primes

In the case that
p
is a prime of ordinary reduction for
E
we also improve on some of the existing results towards
the
p
-part of the BSD conjecture and the
p
-converse for
E
.

p
-part of the BSD formula, bis

We prove:

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
, and
p\nmid 2N
an ordinary prime.
Suppose that the following holds.

(irr
Q
)

The mod
p
Galois representation
\overline{\rho}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Aut}}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}E[p]
is absolutely irreducible.

(ram)

There exists a prime
\ell||N
such that
\overline{\rho}
is ramified at
\ell
.

If
{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=1}L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=1
, then the
p
-part of the BSD formula holds, i.e.

\bigg|\frac{L^{\prime}(1,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})}{\Omega_{E}R(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})}\bigg|_{p}^{-1}=\bigg|\#\hbox{\cyr X}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})\cdot\prod_{q|N}c_{q}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})\bigg|_{p}^{-1}

for
\Omega_{E}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
the period and
R(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
the regulator.

The hypothesis (ram) is not satisfied by CM curves, however our approach also applies to the CM case; see Theorem
K.12
.

p
-converse to the Gross–Zagier and Kolyvagin theorem, bis

We prove:

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
, and
p\nmid 2N
an ordinary prime.
Suppose that the following holds.

(sur
Q
)

The mod
p
Galois representation
\overline{\rho}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Aut}}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}E[p]
is surjective.

(ram)

There exists a prime
\ell||N
such that
\overline{\rho}
is ramified at
\ell
.

Then

{\mathrm{corank}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathrm{Sel}}_{p^{\infty}}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=1\implies{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=1}L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=1.

Remark 1.11
.

In both Theorems
1.9
and
1.10
the hypothesis (ram) can be replaced with the existence of an auxiliary real quadratic field as in
[
140
, Thm. 4]
.

1.1.3.
Perrin-Riou’s conjecture

In the early 90’s Rubin
[
122
]
established a link between elliptic units and rational points on a CM elliptic curve. Shortly later, Perrin-Riou
[
112
]
proposed a conjectural generalisation of this phenomemenon to arbitrary elliptic curves.

For an elliptic curve
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
and a prime
p
, put
V=T_{p}(E)\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

Let
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)
be the Bloch–Kato subgroup.
Let

z_{E}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},V)

be the
p
-adic Beilinson–Kato element arising from the image of the Beilinson–Kato element
{\bf{z}}_{\gamma}(f_{E})
in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},V)
(cf. §
3.2.1
) with
f_{E}
the associated elliptic newform and
\gamma
as in §
2.2.7
.
The Belinson–Kato elements are constructed from special elements in the
K_{2}
groups of modular curves, whose definition relies on Siegel units.
By Kato’s explicit reciprocity law,

{\mathrm{loc}}_{p}(z_{E})\in H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)\iff L(1,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=0

for
{\mathrm{loc}}_{p}:H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},V)\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)
the localisation (cf.
[
79
, Thm. 12.5]
).
Note that
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)=E({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

In the case
{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=1}L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})>0
Perrin-Riou conjectured
the
p
-adic Beilinson–Kato element is linked with the arithmetic of
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
as follows.

Conjecture 1.12
.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be an elliptic curve and

p
a prime.
Let
z_{E}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},V)
be the associated
p
-adic Beilinson–Kato element as above.
Suppose that
L(1,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=0.

Then there exists
P\in E({\mathbb{Q}})
with the following properties.

(a)

For
\omega
a Néron differential,

\log_{\omega}:E({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\rightarrow{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
the
p
-adic logarithm, we have

\log_{\omega}({\mathrm{loc}}_{p}(z_{E}))\doteq\log_{\omega}(P)^{2},

where ‘
\doteq
’ is equality up to
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
-multiple.

(b)

Moreover,

0\neq P\in E({\mathbb{Q}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{Q}}\iff{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=1}L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=1.

In particular,

{\mathrm{loc}}_{p}(z_{E})\neq 0\iff{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=1}L(s,E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=1.

Our main result towards Conjecture
1.12
is the following

Theorem 1.13
.

Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
, and

p\nmid 2N
a prime. If
p=3
and
E
has supersingular reduction at 3, suppose that
(
1.7
) holds.
Then Conjecture
1.12
is true for the pair
(E,p)
.

In the text we prove a
p
-integral version of Conjecture
1.12
(cf. (
7.4
)).

1.2.
The zeta element

We describe our results on the existence of a zeta element for an elliptic curve together with an imaginary quadratic field.

1.2.1.
Setting

Let
E
be an elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}
of conductor
N
, and
p\nmid 2N
a prime.
Let
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
be an algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{Q}}
. Fix embeddings
\iota_{\infty}:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
and
\iota_{p}:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
.

Let
L
be an imaginary quadratic field and
O_{L}
its ring of integers.
Suppose that
1
1

1

The main text allows
D_{L}
and
N
to have some common divisors.

(D_{L},N)=1

(1.3)

and

(p)=v\overline{v}
splits in
L
with
v
determined via
\iota_{p}
.

(1.4)

Let
L_{\infty}
be the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{2}
-extension of
L
and
\Gamma_{L}={\mathrm{Gal}}(L_{\infty}/L)
.
Put
\Lambda_{L}={\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\![\Gamma_{L}]\!]
and
\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}=\Lambda_{L}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}W(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
, viewed as
G_{L}:={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}/L)
-modules via the canonical projection
G_{L}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{L}
.

We often assume that

E[p](L)=0

(1.5)

or

E[p]
is an irreducible
G_{L}
-module.

(1.6)

The latter is satisfied for any supersingular prime
p>2
. Note that the former allows
p
to be an Eisenstein prime.

The nature of the zeta element for
E
relies on the type of reduction at
p
.
If
E
has ordinary reduction at
p
, then there exists an exact sequence

0\rightarrow T^{+}\rightarrow T\rightarrow T^{-}\rightarrow 0

of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}]
-modules, where

T
denotes the
p
-adic Tate module of
E
and

T^{-}
is an unramified
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}]
-module,
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-free of rank one.
In the supersingular case we assume

a_{p}(E):=p+1-\#E({\mathbb{F}}_{p})=0,

(1.7)

referring to
p
as supersingular. This is automatic for
p\geq 5
.

For an ordinary or a supersingular prime
p\nmid 2N
and
\bullet\in\{+,-\}
, put

\circ=\begin{cases}{\mathrm{ord}}&\text{$p$ is ordinary}\\
\bullet&\text{$p$ is supersingular}\end{cases}\ \cdot=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{$p$ is ordinary}\\
\circ&\text{$p$ is supersingular.}\end{cases}

(1.8)

The zeta element lives in the Galois cohomology group

H^{1}_{\mathrm{rel},\circ}({\mathbb{Z}}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L})=\{\kappa\in H^{1}(O_{L}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L})\ :\ {\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{v}}(\kappa)\in H^{1}_{\circ}(L_{\overline{v}},T(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L})\}.

In the ordinary case
H^{1}_{\circ}(L_{\overline{v}},T(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L}):={\mathrm{Im}}\{H^{1}(L_{\overline{v}},T^{+}(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L})\rightarrow H^{1}(L_{\overline{v}},T(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L})\}
. In the supersingular case the definition of

H^{1}_{\circ}(L_{\overline{v}},T(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L})
is based on a principle of Kobayashi.

Let

{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\rm Gr}(E_{/L})\in\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}

be the associated Rankin–Selberg
p
-adic
L
-function interpolating the algebraic part of
L
-values
L(1,E_{/L}\otimes\chi)
for certain infinite order Hecke characters
\chi
over
L
, referred to as a Greenberg
p
-adic
L
-function.
Note that it is a bounded measure independent of the type of reduction of
E
at
p
.
The following
p
-adic
L
-function does depend on the reduction. For
\cdot
as in (
1.8
), let

{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})\in\Lambda_{L}

be the associated Rankin–Selberg
p
-adic
L
-function interpolating a corresponding normalisation of the algebraic part of
L
-values
L(1,E_{L}\otimes\chi)
for finite order characters
\chi
of
\Gamma_{L}
.

A conjecture of Kato
[
77
,
78
]
predicts the existence of a zeta element in
H^{1}_{\mathrm{rel},\circ}(O_{L}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L})
intertwined with the
p
-adic
L
-functions
{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\rm Gr}(E_{/L})
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})
.

1.2.2.

The central result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 1.14
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
{\mathbb{Q}}
of conductor
N
and
p\nmid 2N
a prime. In the case
p=3
suppose that (
1.7
) holds if
p
is supersingular. Let
L
be an imaginary quadratic field satisfying the conditions (
1.3
), (
1.4
) and (
1.5
). Then there exists a zeta element

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})\in H^{1}_{{\mathrm{rel}},\circ}(O_{L}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\hat{\otimes}\Lambda_{L})

such that

{\rm Col}_{v}^{\cdot}({\mathrm{loc}}_{v}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})))={\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\cdot}(E_{/L}),\ {\rm Log}_{\overline{v}}^{\cdot}({\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{v}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})))={\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\rm Gr}(E_{/L}).

Here
{\rm Col}_{v}^{\cdot}:H^{1}(L_{v},T(1)\otimes\Lambda_{L})\rightarrow\Lambda_{L}
and

{\rm Log}_{\overline{v}}^{\cdot}:H^{1}_{\circ}(L_{\overline{v}},T(1)\otimes\Lambda_{L})\rightarrow\Lambda_{L}
are Perrin-Riou regulator maps interpolating Bloch–Kato dual exponential and logarithm maps as in sections
5
and
6
.

Remark 1.15
.

(i)

The hypotheses (
1.3
) and (
1.5
) are not essential. Without them, the explicit reciprocity laws merely have a slightly different appearance.

(ii)

Since the
p
-adic
L
-functions
{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\rm Gr}(E_{/L})
are non-zero, so is the zeta element

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})
.

1.3.
About the proofs

We begin by explaining the connections of the zeta element with the arithmetic of elliptic curves,
and then we give an outline of our proof of its existence.

1.3.1.
The zeta element and main conjectures

The zeta element is ancillary to the Iwasawa theory of elliptic curves over imaginary quadratic fields, leading to
the equivalence of apparently distant main conjectures.

Main conjectures with
p
-adic
L
-functions

Conjecture 1.16
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
{\mathbb{Q}}
of conductor
N
and
p\nmid 2N
a prime. In the case
p=3
suppose that (
1.7
) holds if
p
is supersingular. Let
L
be an imaginary quadratic field satisfying the conditions (
1.3
), (
1.4
) and (
1.5
).
Then

(a)

X_{\cdot}(E_{/L})
is
\Lambda_{L}
-torsion, and

(b)

(\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\cdot}(E_{/L}))=\xi_{\Lambda_{L}}(X_{\cdot}(E_{/L})),

an equality of ideals in
\Lambda_{L}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and even in
\Lambda_{L}
if (
1.6
) holds.

For the definition of the Selmer group
X_{\cdot}(E_{/L})
, the reader may refer to subsection
I.1.2
.

Conjecture 1.17
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
{\mathbb{Q}}
of conductor
N
and
p\nmid 2N
a prime.
Let
L
be an imaginary quadratic field satisfying the conditions (
1.3
) and (
1.4
).
Then

(a)

X_{\rm{Gr}}(E_{/L})
is
\Lambda_{L}
-torsion, and

(b)

(\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\rm{Gr}}(E_{/L}))=\xi_{\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}(X_{\rm{Gr}}(E_{/L})),

an equality of ideals in
\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}
.

For the definition of the Selmer group
X_{\rm Gr}(E_{/L})
, the reader may refer to subsection
I.1.2
.

Zeta element main conjecture

Conjecture 1.18
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
{\mathbb{Q}}
of conductor
N
and
p\nmid 2N
a prime. In the case
p=3
suppose that (
1.7
) holds if
p
is supersingular. Let
L
be an imaginary quadratic field satisfying the conditions (
1.3
), (
1.4
) and (
1.5
).

Let

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})\in H^{1}_{\rm{rel},\circ}(L,T(1)\otimes\Lambda_{L})

be the
corresponding two-variable zeta element.
Then

(a)

X_{{\mathrm{st}},\circ}(E_{/L})
is
\Lambda_{L}
-torsion, and

(b)

\xi\big(H^{1}_{\rm{rel},\circ}(L,T(1)\otimes\Lambda_{L})/\Lambda_{L}\cdot{{\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})}\big)=\xi_{\Lambda_{L}}(X_{{\mathrm{st}},\circ}(E_{/L})),

an equality of ideals in
\Lambda_{L}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and even in

\Lambda_{L,\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}
if (
1.6
) holds.

The explicit reciprocity law as in Theorem
1.14
leads to the following.

Proposition 1.19
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
{\mathbb{Q}}
of conductor
N
and
p\nmid 2N
a prime. In the case
p=3
suppose that (
1.7
) holds if
p
is supersingular. Let
L
be an imaginary quadratic field satisfying the conditions (
1.3
), (
1.4
) and (
1.5
).
Then a one-sided divisibility in any of the Conjectures
1.16
,
1.17
and
1.18

implies the analogous divisibility in the other conjectures.
In particular, these conjectures are equivalent.

1.3.2.
Kobayashi’s main conjecture: deducing Theorem
1.3

Let the setting be as in §
1.1.1
.

For a suitable choice of an imaginary quadratic field
L
, we first deduce the divisibility

\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\rm{Gr}}(E_{/L})\big|\xi_{\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}(X_{\rm{Gr}}^{\rm ur}(E_{/L}))

from the results of
[
141
,
39
]
, which rely on the
Eisenstein congruence method on the unitary group
U(3,1)
. The zeta element then yields the divisibility

\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\circ}(E_{/L})\big|\xi_{\Lambda_{L}}(X_{\circ}(E_{/L}))

by Proposition
1.19
. Considering the cyclotomic projection, we obtain

\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\circ}(E)\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\circ}(E^{L})\big|\xi_{\Lambda}(X_{\circ}(E))\xi_{\Lambda}(X_{\circ}(E^{L}))

for
E^{L}
the quadratic twist of
E
corresponding to the extension
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
.

On the other hand, based on the work of Kato, Kobayashi proved that

\xi_{\Lambda}(X_{\circ}(E^{\prime}))\big|{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\circ}(E^{\prime})

for
E^{\prime}\in\{E,E^{L}\}
(cf.
[
87
]
). Hence all of the above divisibilities are equalities, concluding the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Remark 1.20
.

The proof of the
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
-main conjecture in the ordinary case in
[
131
]
relied on the
U(2,2)
-Eisenstein congruence for Hida families. Attempts to generalise it to the supersingular case seem to need new ideas.
The
U(3,1)
-Eisenstein congruence for certain semi-ordinary Hida families and the zeta element
lead to the main conjecture in both the ordinary and supersingular case.

1.3.3.
Perrin-Riou’s conjecture: proof

Our approach is based on the two-variable zeta element, a variant of Beilinson–Kato elements, and the
p
-adic Waldspurger formula of Bertolini–Darmon–Prasanna.

We give an outline in the case
\epsilon(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})=-1
.
Let
L
be an imaginary quadratic field satisfying (
1.4
) such that each prime dividing
N
splits in
L
, and so

\epsilon(E_{/L})=-1
.

To begin, building on
[
79
]
, we introduce a
Beilinson–Kato element over
L
:

{\bf{z}}^{\cdot}_{E_{/L}}\in H^{1}_{{\rm rel},\circ}(O_{L}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{cyc}}})

for
\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
the cyclotomic Iwasawa algebra.
It is a
\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
-linear combination of the cyclotomic Beilinson–Kato elements

{\bf{z}}_{E}
and

{\bf{z}}_{E^{L}}

(
{\bf{z}}_{E}
is a cyclotomic deformation
2
2

2

In fact, Kato first constructs the
\Lambda
-adic zeta element
{\bf{z}}_{E}
and then defines
z_{E}
.
of the Beilinson–Kato element
z_{E}
).
The explicit reciprocity law for
{\bf{z}}^{\cdot}_{E_{/L}}
at the place
v
yields the cyclotomic
p
-adic
L
-function, interpolating the central
L
-values of the twists of
E_{/L}

by the finite order Hecke characters along the cyclotomic tower.

On the other hand, we have the two-variable zeta element
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})
.
Its explicit reciprocity law at the place
\overline{v}
in combination with the
p
-adic Waldspurger formula gives

\log_{\overline{v}}({\mathds{1}}_{L}({\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{v}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L}))))\doteq(\log_{\omega}y_{L})^{2}

(1.9)

for
\log_{\overline{v}}
the Bloch–Kato logarithm,

\mathds{1}_{L}(\cdot)
the specialisation at the identity Hecke character,
and
y_{L}\in E(L)
a Heegner point arising from a modular parametrisation of
E
.

Hence, in view of (
1.9
) Conjecture
1.12
amounts to the comparison

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})^{{\mathrm{cyc}}}\doteq{\bf{z}}_{E_{/L}}

(1.10)

of cyclotomic zeta elements (here
(\cdot)^{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
is the cyclotomic projection).
We first establish a local analogue of (
1.10
) at the place
v
via the explicit reciprocity laws for
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})
and

{\bf{z}}_{E_{/L}}
at
v
.
The comparison (
1.10
) then follows from the key global fact:

{\mathrm{rank}}_{\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{cyc}}}}H^{1}_{{\rm rel},\circ}(O_{L}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\Lambda_{L}^{{\mathrm{cyc}}})=1,

which is essentially due to Kato
[
79
]
.

Actually, the zeta element
{\bf z}_{E_{/L}}^{\cdot}
need not be
p
-integral, in which case we slightly modify the strategy.

1.3.4.
Zeta elements over imaginary quadratic fields: existence

Our construction of the zeta element builds on the fundamental progress on Rankin--Selberg zeta elements
3
3

3

More specifically, the work of Lei–Loeffler–Zerbes
[
93
]
and its generalizations
[
94
,
85
,
98
,
28
]
are foundational to our construction of the zeta element.

due to Bertolini–Darmon–Rotger
[
12
,
13
]
, Lei–Loeffler–Zerbes
[
93
,
94
]
, Kings–Loeffler–Zerbes
[
85
]
, Loeffler–Zerbes
[
98
]
and Buyukboduk–Lei
[
28
]
.
While these works initiate the construction, an essential difficulty arises from the presence of an Eisenstein CM Hida family for which the
p
-distinguished hypothesis fails, and the pertinent Hecke algebra is non-Gorenstein.
This is reflected in the occurrence of singularities in the geometric construction of the zeta element, and necessitates a fine analysis of the global geometry underlying the construction, especially of the Tate lattice associated to the CM Hida family. Our analysis is quite roundabout, based on Beilinson–Kato elements and
auxiliary Rankin–Selberg zeta elements!

We now outline the construction in the ordinary case. Let
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
and
p\nmid 2N
an ordinary prime. Let
L
be an imaginary quadratic field satisfying (
1.3
), (
1.4
) and (
1.5
).

Let
{\bf h}_{v}
be the canonical CM family passing through the weight one theta series
\theta(\chi_{L})
associated to the quadratic character
\chi_{L}
of the extension
L/{\mathbb{Q}}

with coefficients in the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda_{L}^{v}
of the maximal
p
-abelian extension of
L
unramified outside
v
as in

4.1.6
.
Note that

\theta(\chi_{L})
is Eisenstein, in the sense that its associated mod
p
residual Galois representation is reducible, hence in turn so is
{\bf h}_{v}
.
The Tate lattices
4
4

4

in the sense of Hida
associated with the canonical CM family
{\bf{h}}_{v}
are the
\Lambda_{L}^{v}[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}]
-modules

{\mathbb{T}}=H^{1}_{{\mathrm{ord}}}(D_{L}p^{\infty})\otimes_{{\mathbb{T}}_{D_{L}p^{\infty}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}},\varphi}\Lambda_{L}^{v}\ \ \text{and}\ \ {\mathbb{H}}={\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\mathrm{ord}}(D_{L}p^{\infty})\otimes_{{\mathbb{H}}_{D_{L}p^{\infty}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}},\varphi}\Lambda_{L}^{v}.

Here
{\mathbb{T}}_{D_{L}p^{\infty}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
(resp.
{\mathbb{H}}_{D_{L}p^{\infty}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
) is the cuspidal (resp. full) Hida Hecke algebra of tame level
D_{L}
, and
H^{1}_{{\mathrm{ord}}}(D_{L}p^{\infty})
(resp.
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\mathrm{ord}}(D_{L}p^{\infty})
) is the ordinary part of the étale cohomology of the tower of closed (resp. open) modular curves, and
\varphi:{\mathbb{H}}_{D_{L}p^{\infty}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{T}}_{D_{L}p^{\infty}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{L}^{v}
is the homomorphism corresponding to the Hecke eigenform
{\bf h}_{v}
. Note that the Hecke algebras
{\mathbb{H}}_{D_{L}p^{\infty}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{D_{L}p^{\infty}}^{{\mathrm{ord}}}
, as well as the Tate modules
{\mathbb{H}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}
,
are distinct since
{\bf h}_{v}
is Eisenstein.
Moreover, a priori, neither
{\mathbb{T}}
nor
{\mathbb{H}}
are free
\Lambda_{L}^{v}
-modules. Put
{\mathbb{H}}_{1}={\mathbb{H}}/{\mathbb{H}}_{{\mathrm{tor}}}
.

Let

\mathcal{BF}(E_{/L})\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Z}}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\hat{\otimes}{\mathbb{H}}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\Lambda)

be the image of the Beilinson–Flach element
\mathcal{BF}({{f_{E}},{{\mathbf{h}}}})

associated
5
5

5

Here the coefficients involve the open Tate lattice
{\mathbb{H}}_{1}
, instead of the closed lattice
{\mathbb{T}}
, since the construction relies on Siegel units which are supported on cusps.
to the
p
-ordinary stabilisation of the weight two newform attached to
E
and the CM Hida family
{\bf h}_{v}
. The corresponding explicit reciprocity laws
[
93
,
85
]
link it to the
p
-adic
L
-functions
{\mathcal{L}}_{p}(E_{/L})
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\rm Gr}(E_{/L})
. Hence one expects

\mathcal{BF}(E_{/L})
to be a candidate for the zeta element
{\mathcal{Z}}(E_{/L})
.
In fact, the former is a Rankin–Selberg zeta element over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, and it would apparently be the desired zeta element for
E
over
L
if
{\mathbb{H}}_{1}
were integrally induced
from
G_{L}
as a
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-representation. However, an essential obstacle is that
{\mathbb{H}}_{1}
need not be even
\Lambda_{L}^{v}
-free!

Our construction of the zeta element from
\mathcal{BF}(E_{/L})
takes a different route, the key being the following.

Theorem 1.21
.

(a)

We have

\mathcal{BF}(E_{/L})\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Z}}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\hat{\otimes}{\mathbb{T}}\hat{\otimes}\Lambda)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Z}}[\frac{1}{p}],T(1)\hat{\otimes}{\mathbb{H}}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\Lambda).

(b)

The Tate lattice
{\mathbb{T}}
is integrally induced, i.e. there exists an isomorphism

{\mathbb{T}}\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}_{G_{L}}^{G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}}(\Lambda_{L}^{v}(\Theta_{v}))

of
\Lambda[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}]
-modules for

\Theta_{v}
the Hecke character as in (
4.5
).

In light of this theorem and the explicit reciprocity laws,

\mathcal{BF}(E_{/L})
does lead to the sought after zeta element
{\mathcal{Z}}(E_{/L})
.

Note that parts (a) and (b) of Theorem
1.21
have a markedly different appearance, yet their proofs are intertwined!
A satisfying explanation that zeta element arises from the Tate lattice of the closed curve, as in part (a), has eluded us.

In the absence of the
p
-distinguished hypothesis present methods to study
\Lambda
-adic Tate modules do not apply.
Our roundabout approach to the proof of Theorem
1.21
is based on Rankin–Selberg zeta elements for the pairs
(g,{\bf{h}}_{v})
for
g
a
p
-ordinary weight two newform:
we introduce a preliminary two-variable zeta element for
g
over
L
using the former zeta element
and an analysis of the reflexive closure of the

\Lambda
-adic Tate modules.
Heuristically, this construction involves resolution of singularities arising from the underlying non-Gorenstein-ness.
Recall that the Rankin–Selberg zeta element lives in the first Iwasawa cohomology of the

\Lambda_{L}^{v}[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}]
-module
T_{g}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{H}}_{1}
for
T_{g}
a Tate module associated to
g
(cf.
[
93
,
85
]
).
Since the maximal torsion-free quotient
{\mathbb{H}}_{1}
need not be a free
\Lambda_{L}^{v}
-module, we are lead to consider its reflexive closure
\tilde{{\mathbb{H}}}
and the image of the zeta element in
T_{g}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\tilde{{\mathbb{H}}}
. The analysis of the reflexive closure
relies on Ohta’s work, especially
[
108
]
.

If the property (b) fails, we show that an explicit reciprocity law for the preliminary zeta element over
L

would
contradict Theorem
1.14
(with
E
replaced by
g
).
Here we utilise results regarding congruence ideal for the canonical CM Hida family due to Bellaiche–Dimitrov
[
15
]
and
Betina–Dimitrov–Pozzi
[
16
]
, as well as Beilinson–Kato elments.
In view of such an anomalous explicit reciprocity law the Greenberg
p
-adic
L
-function
{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\rm Gr}(g_{/L})

would vanish at the identity Hecke character over
L
for
any

p
-ordinary weight two newform
g
.
We arrive at a contradiction by constructing an
auxiliary
newform
g
for a given imaginary quadratic field
L
for which
{\mathcal{L}}_{p}^{\rm Gr}(g_{/L})
does not vanish at the identity Hecke character.
The construction is based on
the anticyclotomic non-vanishing results of Rohrlich
[
119
]

and the
p
-adic Waldspurger formula.

Our proof of part (a) is based on (b) and a variant of the above strategy.

The construction in the supersingular case follows along similar lines, though we now have part (b) of Theorem
1.21
at our disposal.

1.4.
Vistas

1.4.1.
Euler systems over imaginary quadratic fields

In a forth-coming companion paper we expect to show that the two-variable zeta element extends to an Euler system, leading to a
p
-adic Euler system for elliptic curves
E
over imaginary quadratic fields
L
as in Theorem
1.14
. The method is based on a refinement of the strategy outlined in

1.3.4
.

1.4.2.
Euler systems for
{\mathrm{GSp}}_{4}\times{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}

Our study of the
\Lambda
-adic Tate modules, especially Theorem
1.21
(b), seems to be
relevant in the context of Euler system for
{\mathrm{GSp}}_{4}\times{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
(cf.
[
74
,
99
]
). It may shed some light on the existence of zeta elements for modular abelian surfaces over imaginary quadratic fields.

1.4.3.
Conjectures of Mazur, Kato, and Kolyvagin

The two-variable zeta element has other applications. It is a key ingredient in the proof of Mazur’s main conjecture at Eisenstein primes
[
38
]
. It is also crucial in the recent proof of Kato’s main conjecture and Perrin-Riou’s Heegner point main conjecture at primes of good reduction under mild conditions
[
8
]
, and in turn of Kolyvagin’s conjecture
[
6
]
. It is also an ingredient in the recent proof of a result towards Kato’s main conjecture at primes of additive reduction
[
56
]
.
Some complementary application appear in
[
31
,
34
,
36
]
.

1.4.4.
Sharifi’s conjecture

It would be interesting to situate our results regarding the
\Lambda
-adic Tate modules and zeta elements (cf. Theorems
1.14
and
1.21
) in the context of Sharifi’s conjectures
[
126
]
. These conjectures pertain to
\Lambda
-adic Tate modules in the Eisenstein case. However, the current framework assumes a Gorenstein or cyclicity hypothesis and excludes our setting.

1.5.
Related results

We include a few remarks about related results in the literature (cf.
[
25
]
).

p
-part of the BSD formula

For semistable elliptic curves satisfying (irr
Q
), the
p
-part of the BSD formula in the analytic rank one case is also due to Jetchev–Skinner–Wan with
p
a prime of good reduction
[
76
]
and Skinner–Zhang
[
132
]
and Castella
[
33
]
with
p>3
a prime of multiplicative reduction. Note that the hypothesis (ram) automatically holds in the semistable case.
The method of Jetchev–Skinner–Wan relies on the
p
-adic Waldspurger formula
[
11
]
.
For supersingular primes, it gives a different approach to the
r=1
case of Theorem
1.5
. It is independent of the
p
-adic Gross–Zagier formula, but relies on
Theorem
1.3
.

Several cases of Theorem
1.9
have been obtained by Zhang
[
149
]

and Berti–Bertolini–Venerucci
[
5
]
. The results are conditional on the surjectivity of
\overline{\rho}
and impose restrictions on primes
q|N
at which
p
is allowed to divide the Tamagawa number
c_{q}(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})
.
The case of Eisenstein primes
p
appears in
[
38
]
(see also
[
24
]
).
An analogue holds for CM elliptic curves under milder hypothesis, for example
p
can be a prime of good supersingular reduction.
The result is originally due to Rubin
[
123
]
and Kobayashi
[
91
]
. We provide another proof.

In the analytic rank zero case, Kato proved that the
p
-part of the BSD formula is a consequence of Kato’s main conjecture.
We show that an analogous phenomenon persists in the analytic rank one case (cf. §
K.3
).

The results are conditional on the surjectivity of

An analogue holds for CM elliptic curves under milder hypothesis, for example

Perrin-Riou’s conjecture

The split multiplicative case of Conjecture
1.12

Some cases of Conjecture

The hypothesis
1.7

The recent preprint
[
133
]
adapts the strategy of
[
143
]
(that is, the strategy fully realised in this paper) to the case
a_{p}\neq 0
and not a
p
-adic unit together with the author’s construction
of ‘signed’ bounded
p
-adic
L
-functions in this case. This existence of a suitable variant of the zeta elements
\mathcal{Z}^{\cdot}(E_{/L})
in this case would then yield corresponding main conjectures. Unfortunately, the existence of these zeta elements is not immediate from our constructions. While their existence seems plausible to us, less clear is that they would be a straightforward consequence of the methods employed herein.

1.6.
Structure of the paper

In part I we study zeta elements associated to a weight two elliptic newform and an imaginary quadratic field
L
, namely
a one-variable Beilinson–Kato element over
L
(cf. §
3.2.3
) and a two-variable zeta element over
L
(cf. §
5.5
and
6.6
).
A link among the two is central to the paper.
The analysis of a
\Lambda
-adic Tate module associated to a residually reducible CM Hida family with CM by
L
constitutes a significant part (cf. §
4
–§
5
). The analysis is closely tied with the existence of the two variable zeta element over
L
.
This part concludes with the proof of the Perrin-Riou conjecture (cf. §
7.2
).

In part II we study certain Iwasawa main conjectures and the BSD conjecture
for the elliptic newform over the rationals and the imaginary quadratic field
L
. The cyclotomic Greenberg main conjecture over the imaginary quadratic field (cf. §
I.2.2
) is key. In view of the comparison of the zeta elements, a recent result towards the two-variable Greenberg main conjecture over
L
leads to a proof of Kobayashi’s main conjecture at supersingular primes, with concomitant applications to the BSD conjecture (cf. §
J
).
In a similar vein, we establish the cyclotomic Greenberg main conjecture under a non-vanishing hypothesis
based on the work of Kato, Skinner–Urban and Rubin (for example, see Corollary
I.23
).
In view of the
p
-adic Waldspurger formula, the hypothesis can be verified in certain rank one situations (cf. (
K.3
)).
This leads to results towards the BSD conjecture at ordinary primes (cf. §
K
–§
L
).

Part I
Zeta elements

2.
Notation and preliminaries

In this section we recall the various objects, especially newforms,
L
-functions, periods, etc., which are fundamental
to the main results of this paper, introducing notation that will be in force throughout.

2.1.
Some notation

We begin with some general notation.

2.1.1.
The prime
p

Throughout
p\geq 3
will be an odd prime. Some results will also hold even for
p=2
, but restricting to odd
p
simplifies
many arguments.
Some of the main results of this paper are only stated for
p\geq 5
. When appropriate we include commments on this restriction.

2.1.2.
The embeddings
\iota_{\infty}
and
\iota_{p}

Let
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a fixed separable algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
a fixed separable algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Let
\iota_{\infty}:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
and
\iota_{p}:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
be fixed embeddings of fields.
Via
\iota_{\infty}
, the complex conjugation
\tau\in{\mathrm{Gal}}({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}})
induces an element of order two in
G_{\mathbb{Q}}={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{\mathbb{Q}})
, which is also denoted by
\tau
.
The embedding
\iota_{p}
identifies
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
with a subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

2.1.3.
Subfields of
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}

For a subfield
K\subset\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
, let
G_{K}={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}/K)
.
For a set of places
\Sigma
of
K
, let
K_{\Sigma}\subset\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
be the maximal extension of
K
unramified outside
\Sigma
and let

G_{K,\Sigma}={\mathrm{Gal}}(K_{\Sigma}/K)
.
For a place
w
of
K
, let
\overline{K}_{w}
denote a fixed algebraic closure of
K_{w}
and let
G_{K_{w}}={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{K}_{w}/K_{w})
.
Let
I_{w}\subset G_{K_{w}}
be the inertia subgroup.
In the case that the residue field of
K_{w}
is finite, let
{\mathrm{Frob}}_{w}\in G_{K_{w}}/I_{w}
denote an
arithmetic
Frobenius.
For convenience, we fix a
K
-linear embedding
\iota_{w}:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow\overline{K}_{w}
, so that
G_{K_{w}}
is identified with a decomposition group for
w
in
G_{K}
.
For
K={\mathbb{Q}}
and
w=p
we take
\iota_{p}
for this embedding.

2.1.4.
The character
\epsilon

We write
\epsilon
for the
p
-adic cyclotomic character
\epsilon:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
.

2.1.5.
The Iwasawa algebras
\Lambda_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\Lambda

Let
{\mathbb{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
be the cyclotomic extension obtained by adjoining all
p
-power
roots of unity and let
{\mathcal{G}}={\mathrm{Gal}}({\mathbb{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})/{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Let
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\infty}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
be the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}
and let
\Gamma={\mathrm{Gal}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{\infty}/{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Let
\Delta={\mathrm{Gal}}({\mathbb{Q}}(\mu_{p})/{\mathbb{Q}})
. Then the canonical projections of
{\mathcal{G}}
to
\Gamma
and
\Delta
induce a
canonical isomorphism
{\mathcal{G}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma\times\Delta
, which we use to identify
{\mathcal{G}}
with the product

\Gamma\times\Delta
.
Let

\Lambda_{\mathcal{G}}={\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\![{\mathcal{G}}]\!]\ \ \text{and}\ \ \Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\![\Gamma]\!].

More generally, for a
p
-adically complete
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra
R
, let

\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}
(resp.
\Lambda_{R}
) denote
R[\![{\mathcal{G}}]\!]
(resp.
R[\![\Gamma]\!]
).
Note that
\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}
is naturally a
\Lambda_{R}
-module and
\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}
is identified with
\Lambda_{R}[\Delta]
.
Let
\gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}\in\Gamma
a topological generator. Then we have an
R
-isomorphism

\Lambda_{R}\xrightarrow{\sim}R[\![T]\!]
arising from
\gamma_{\mathrm{cyc}}\mapsto 1+T
.

Let
\omega:\Delta\xrightarrow{\sim}({\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}})^{\times}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mu_{p-1}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}

be the composition of the canonical isomorphism with the Teichmüller lift. For an integer
i
, let

\varepsilon_{i}=\frac{1}{\#\Delta}\sum_{\sigma\in\Delta}\omega^{-i}(\sigma)\sigma\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\Delta]

be the idempotent associated with
\omega^{i}
.
Then
\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}
decomposes as

\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}=\oplus_{i=0}^{p-2}\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}^{(i)}
with

\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}^{(i)}=\varepsilon_{i}\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}=\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}\varepsilon_{i}=\Lambda_{R}\varepsilon_{i}
.
Each
\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}},R}^{(i)}
is a subring canonically isomorphic to
\Lambda_{R}
, with identity element
\varepsilon_{i}
.

The canonical projections

\Psi_{\mathcal{G}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \Psi:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma

(2.1)

are often referred to as the canonical characters and viewed
as taking values in
\Lambda_{\mathcal{G}}^{\times}

and
\Lambda^{\times}
, respectively.
The composition of
\Psi_{\mathcal{G}}
with the projection to
\Lambda_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{(i)}
is
\omega^{i}\Psi
, where we also write

\omega
for the composition
G_{\mathbb{Q}}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
.

2.2.
Newforms

Let
g\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
be a newform of weight two, level
N
, and trivial character. To simplify some arguments, we will always assume that

p\nmid N

(2.2)

from §
2.2.6
onward.

2.2.1.
The Hecke field of a newform

Let
g(q)=\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{g}(n)q^{n}
be the
q
-expansion (
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
) of the newform
g
. Recall that the field

F={\mathbb{Q}}(a_{g}(n);n\geq 1),

the Hecke field of
g
, is a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}
, and

\mathcal{O}_{F,0}={\mathbb{Z}}[a_{g}(n);n\geq 1]\subset F
is an order (possibly not maximal). Let
\mathcal{O}_{F}
be the ring of integers of
F
(the maximal order).
We view
F
as a subfield of
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
via
\iota_{\infty}
.

Let
\lambda\mid p
be the prime of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
determined
7
7

7

This choice is for convenience. All the subsequent
constructions and results for a different choice of a prime
\lambda
can be obtained either by a different choice of the initial
\iota_{p}

or by replacing
g
with a Galois conjugate.
by
\iota_{p}
. Let
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{F,(\lambda)}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{O}_{F,\lambda}
.
Let
\lambda_{0}=\lambda\cap\mathcal{O}_{F,0}
and let
k_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{F,0}/\lambda_{0}
be its residue field. Let
k_{\lambda}=\mathcal{O}/\lambda=\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}/\lambda\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
;
this is a finite extension of
k_{0}
.

2.2.2.
Hecke algebras

Let
\Gamma
be either
\Gamma_{0}(N)
or
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
Let
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma}
(resp.
{\mathbb{H}}_{\Gamma}
) be the Hecke algebra for level
\Gamma
generated over
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
by the Hecke operators
T(n)
,

n\geq 1
, defined in
[
79
, §4.9]
,
acting on the space of cuspforms
S_{2}(\Gamma)
(resp. the space of modular forms
M_{2}(\Gamma)
).
Similarly, let
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbb{H}}_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
be the Hecke algebras for level
\Gamma
generated over
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
by the dual Hecke operators
T^{\prime}(n)
, also defined in
[
79
, §4.9]
.
The Hecke algebras
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma}
and
{\mathbb{T}}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
(resp.
{\mathbb{H}}_{\Gamma}
and
{\mathbb{H}}_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
) act on the cuspforms (resp. modular forms)
of level
\Gamma
over
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
and also on the cohomology of the closed (resp. open) modular curves of level
\Gamma
over
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}

(cf.
[
79
, §4.9]
).

2.2.3.
Congruence numbers

Let
\phi_{\Gamma}:{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{F,0}\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
be the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
-homomorphism associated with
g
.
There is a factorisation

\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N)}:{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N)}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{0}(N)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{\Gamma_{0}}}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{F,0}\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)},

with the first morphism being the canonical surjection arising from the inclusion
S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))\subset S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))
.
Let

\mathfrak{p}_{0}
be the kernel of
\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N)}
and let
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
be the kernel of the reduction
of
\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N)}
modulo
\lambda_{0}
. Let
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
be the localization of
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(n)}

at the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.

It will often be more convenient to work with

{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma,\mathcal{O}}={\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}}\mathcal{O}
instead of

{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma}
. The
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
-homomorphism
\phi_{\Gamma}
induces an
\mathcal{O}
-homomorphism

\phi_{\Gamma,\mathcal{O}}:{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma,\mathcal{O}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the kernel of
\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}

and let
\mathfrak{m}
be the kernel of the reduction of
\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}
modulo
\lambda
.
Note that
\mathfrak{p}\cap{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N)}=\mathfrak{p}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{m}\cap{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N)}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.
Let
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
be the localization of
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}
at the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, which is then an
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
-algebra.
The natural map
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
induces an isomorphism
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\otimes_{W(k_{0})}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
where
W(k_{0})
is the ring of Witt vectors for the finite field
k_{0}
.

Let
\pi:{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
be the
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
-morphism arising from
\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}
.
A
congruence number
for
g
is an element
c_{g}\in\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
such that

(c_{g})=\pi({\mathrm{Ann}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\ker(\pi))\subset\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}.

Note that a congruence number
c_{g}
is only uniquely defined up to multiplication
by an element in
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}^{\times}
. However, we can and do choose a congruence number
c_{g}\in\mathcal{O}
, which is
then algebraic and uniquely determined up to multiplication by an element of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. A congruence number in
\mathcal{O}
can also be directly defined as follows.

The ring
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}={\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
is a semi-local Artinian
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
-algebra and so
is canonically identified with the product of its localisations at its (finitely many) maximal ideals. In particular,
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a direct factor
of
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}
. Let
\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}
be the
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
-linear extension of
\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}
.
It then follows that

(c_{g})=\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}({\mathrm{Ann}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}}(\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}})\subset\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}.

Since
{\mathrm{Ann}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}}(\ker(\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}))={\mathrm{Ann}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}}(\ker(\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
,
we then also have that a congruence number can be taken to be a generator of the
\mathcal{O}
-ideal

\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}({\mathrm{Ann}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}}(\ker(\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}})))\subset\mathcal{O}
.

2.2.4.
Cohomology of modular curves

Let
Y_{1}(N)
(resp.
Y_{0}(N)
) be the open modular curve of level
\Gamma_{1}(N)
(resp.
\Gamma_{0}(N)
).
Let
X_{1}(N)=X_{1}(N)_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
(resp.
X_{0}(N)
) be the modular curve of level
\Gamma_{1}(N)
(resp.
\Gamma_{0}(N)
). We consider
these as curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}
using the models in
[
79
, §2.8]
.

For a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebra
A
let

V_{A}=\frac{H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),A)}{\mathfrak{p}\cdot H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),A)}.

Here
H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),A)
denotes the Betti cohomology
H^{1}(Y_{1}(N)({\mathbb{C}}),A)
.
The action of the complex conjugation
\tau
induces an involution of
H^{1}(Y_{1}(N)({\mathbb{C}}),A)
and hence of
V_{A}
, which we also denote by
\tau
.
Let
H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),A)^{\pm}
(resp.
V_{A}^{\pm}
) be the submodule on which the action of
\tau
is by multiplication by
\pm 1
.
If
2
is invertible in
A
, then
H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),A)
(resp.
V_{A}
) is a direct sum of
H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),A)^{+}
and
H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),A)^{-}
(resp,
V_{A}^{+}
and
V_{A}^{-}
).

Since
g
is a newform of level
N
,
\dim_{F}V_{F}=2
.
Furthermore, the maps
V_{F}\otimes_{F}F_{\lambda}\rightarrow V_{F_{\lambda}}
and

V_{F}\otimes_{F}{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}}
induced by functoriality are both isomorphisms.
In this way
V_{F}
defines an
F
-structure on each of
V_{F_{\lambda}}
and
V_{\mathbb{C}}
.

The inclusion
\mathcal{O}\hookrightarrow F
induces
an injection
(V_{\mathcal{O}})_{/{\mathrm{tor}}}\hookrightarrow V_{F}
. Let
T_{\mathcal{O}}
be the image of this map.
In particular,

T_{\mathcal{O}}={\mathrm{Im}}(H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})\rightarrow V_{F})={\mathrm{Im}}(V_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow V_{F})

This is an
\mathcal{O}
-lattice in
V_{F}
. We analogously define
T_{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}\subset V_{F_{\lambda}}
.
Under the functorial identification of
V_{F_{\lambda}}
with
V_{F}\otimes_{F}F_{\lambda}
,
T_{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}
is identified
with
T_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
.

2.2.5.
Galois representations

It follows from the comparison isomorphism of Betti and étale cohomology that
V_{F_{\lambda}}

is naturally equipped with a continuous
F_{\lambda}
-linear action of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
and that
T_{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}
is a

G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-stable
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
-lattice in
V_{F_{\lambda}}
. To simplify notation, we will let

V=V_{F_{\lambda}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ T=T_{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}.

The representation

\rho:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Aut}}_{F_{\lambda}}(V)

is unramified at all
\ell\nmid Np
, and for such primes

\mathrm{trace}\,\rho({\mathrm{Frob}}_{\ell}^{-1})=a_{g}(\ell)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \det\rho({\mathrm{Frob}}_{\ell}^{-1})=\ell.

In particular,

\det\rho=\epsilon^{-1}.

Let

\overline{T}=T/\lambda T.

We will sometimes make the following hypothesis relative to a given number field
K\subset\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
:

\overline{T}
is an absolutely irreducible
k_{\lambda}[G_{K}]
-module.

(irr
K
)

This implies, but is not implied by, the hypothesis that for a given abelian extension
M/K:

\overline{T}^{G_{M}}=0.

(van
M
)

2.2.6.
Spaces of modular forms

Since
p\nmid N
, the modular curve
X_{1}(N)_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
extends as a smooth projective scheme
X_{1}(N)_{/{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}}
.
Let
\Omega^{1}_{X_{1}(N)}
be the sheaf of differentials, and
for a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
-algebra
B
let

S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))_{B}=H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{1}(N)})\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}}B.

If
B
is a flat
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
-algebra, then
S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))_{B}=H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/B},\Omega^{1}_{X_{1}(N)})
.
We similarly define
S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))_{B}
.

Let

S_{F}=\frac{S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))_{F}}{\mathfrak{p}\cdot S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))_{F}}.

Since
g
is a newform of level
N
,
\dim_{F}S_{F}=1
.

Let

S_{\mathcal{O}}=\Big(\frac{S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))_{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathfrak{p}\cdot S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))_{\mathcal{O}}}\Big)_{/{\mathrm{tor}}}.

This is a free
\mathcal{O}
-module of rank one, and
the inclusion
\mathcal{O}\hookrightarrow F
identifies
S_{\mathcal{O}}
with
an
\mathcal{O}
-lattice in
S_{F}
.

Good differentials.
We will say that a differential
\omega\in S_{F}
is
good

if it is an
\mathcal{O}
-basis of
S_{\mathcal{O}}
.

2.2.7.
Periods

The Eichler–Shimura period morphism
of
[
79
, §4.10]

induces an injective
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map

per:S_{F}\otimes_{F}{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow V_{F}\otimes_{F}{\mathbb{C}}=V_{\mathbb{C}}.

Fix
\gamma\in V_{F}
with

0\neq\gamma^{\pm}=\frac{1\pm\tau}{2}\cdot\gamma\in V_{F}.

Then
\gamma^{+}
,
\gamma^{-}
are an
F
-basis of
V_{F}
and hence a
{\mathbb{C}}
-basis of
V_{\mathbb{C}}
.
For
0\neq\omega\in S_{F}
, let

\Omega^{\pm}_{\omega,\gamma}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
be
such that

per(\omega)=\Omega^{+}_{\omega,\gamma}\cdot\gamma^{+}+\Omega^{-}_{\omega,\gamma}\cdot\gamma^{-}.

Note that the period
\Omega^{\pm}_{\omega,\gamma}
is uniquely defined only up to multiplication by an element of
F^{\times}
.

Optimal periods.
Since
g
is a newform, the
\mathcal{O}
-modules
H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})^{\pm}[\mathfrak{p}]
are both free of rank one.
Here ‘
[\mathfrak{p}]
’ denotes the submodule which is annihilated by
\mathfrak{p}
.
Let
\delta_{g}^{\pm}\in H^{1}_{c}(Y_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})^{\pm}[\mathfrak{p}]
be
an
\mathcal{O}
-module generator, where the subscript ‘
c
’ denotes compactly supported cohomology. We identify
\delta_{g}^{\pm}
with their images in
V_{F}
.
Similarly, the
\mathcal{O}
-module
H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{1}(N)})[\mathfrak{p}]
is free of rank one.
Let
\omega_{g}\in H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{1}(N)})[\mathfrak{p}]
be
\mathcal{O}
-module generators and let
\delta_{g}=\delta_{g}^{+}+\delta_{g}^{-}\in V_{F}
.
Let
\Omega^{\pm}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
be defined by

per(\omega_{g})=\Omega^{+}\cdot\delta_{g}^{+}+\Omega^{-}\cdot\delta_{g}^{-}.

That is,
\Omega^{\pm}=\Omega^{\pm}_{\omega_{g},\delta_{g}}
.
Note that the
\Omega^{\pm}
are uniquely defined up to
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
-multiples.
The elements
\delta_{g}^{\pm}
map to non-trivial elements in
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{\pm}
and the differential
\omega_{g}
maps to
a non-trivial element of
S_{\mathcal{O}}
, so
\Omega^{\pm}
is an
F^{\times}
-multiple of any
\Omega^{\pm}_{\gamma,\omega}
.
In subsequent considerations, we usually work with these optimal periods
\Omega^{\pm}
.

Remark 2.1
.

If (irr
Q
) holds, then

H^{1}_{c}(Y_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})^{\pm}[\mathfrak{p}]=H^{1}(X_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})^{\pm}[\mathfrak{p}]=H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})^{\pm}[\mathfrak{p}].

Remark 2.2
.

The extent to which the images of
\delta_{g}^{\pm}
and
\omega_{g}
fail to be
\mathcal{O}
-bases can often be measured
by a congruence ideal (see §
2.2.9
below, especially Lemma
2.5
).

Remark 2.3
.

If
g
is the newform associated with an isogeny class of elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, then there is a curve
E_{\bullet}
in the isogeny
class such that the optimal periods
\Omega^{\pm}
are a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
-basis of the lattice of Néron periods of
E_{\bullet}
. If (irr
Q
) holds, this is clear
for
E_{\bullet}=E_{1}
, the optimal quotient of
J_{1}(N)
in the isogeny class. If (irr
Q
) does not hold (in this case,
E_{\bullet}
is a quotient of
E_{1}
by an étale subgroup), this was proved in
[
146
]
, see especially
[
146
, Thm. 4 & Prop. 8]
and their proofs.

2.2.8.
The Gorenstein condition

The ring
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
satisfies the
Gorenstein condition
if

(i)

{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a Gorenstein
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
-algebra, that is,
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}({\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}},\mathcal{O}_{\lambda})
is
a free
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
-module of rank one, and

(ii)

H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a free
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
-module of rank two (equivalently,

H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\pm}
is a free
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
-module of rank one).

If
\mathfrak{m}
is not Eisenstein, in the sense that (irr
Q
) holds,
then (ii) implies (i) (as a consequence of Poincaré duality).
For later reference we record the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4
.

If (irr
Q
) holds, then
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
satisfies the Gorenstein condition.

Proof.

Since
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}={\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathfrak{m_{g}}}\otimes_{W(k_{g})}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
,
this follows immediately from
[
145
, Chap. 2, Cor. 1 and 2]
.
∎

2.2.9.
Integral structures and cuspidal conguence numbers

There are natural injections

H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{1}(N)})[\mathfrak{p}]\hookrightarrow S_{\mathcal{O}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ H^{1}_{c}(Y_{1}(N),\mathcal{O})^{\pm}[\mathfrak{p}]\rightarrow T_{\mathcal{O}}^{\pm}

of free, rank one
\mathcal{O}
-modules.
In particular, there are
c,c^{\pm}\in\mathcal{O}
such that
\frac{\omega_{g}}{c}
is an
\mathcal{O}
-generator of
S_{\mathcal{O}}

and
\gamma_{g}^{\pm}=\frac{\delta_{g}^{\pm}}{c^{\pm}}
is an
\mathcal{O}
-generator of
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{\pm}
.
Such an element
c
is also sometimes called a congruence number for
g
. In order to not
confuse this with the congruence number
c_{g}
defined in §
2.2.3
, we will refer to such a
c
as
a
cuspidal congruence number
for
g
and to the ideal
I_{g,0}=(c)=c\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
as the
cuspidal
congruence ideal
of
g
. Note that
S_{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}=I_{g,0}^{-1}\cdot\omega_{g}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
c_{g}\in\mathcal{O}
be a generator of the congruence ideal for
g
as in §
2.2.3
.

(i)

Each of
c
and
c^{\pm}
divides
c_{g}
.

(ii)

If (irr
Q
) holds, then we can we take
c=c^{\pm}=c_{g}
.
In particular,

S_{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{O}\cdot\omega,\ \ \omega=\frac{\omega_{g}}{c_{g}},

and

T_{\mathcal{O}}^{\pm}=\mathcal{O}\cdot\gamma^{\pm}_{g},\ \ \gamma_{g}^{\pm}=\frac{\delta_{g}^{\pm}}{c_{g}}.

Proof.

Suppose
\omega^{\prime}\in H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)})\subset H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/F},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)})
projects to an
\mathcal{O}
-basis of
S_{\mathcal{O}}
in
S_{F}
. Then

\omega^{\prime}=\frac{\omega_{g}}{c}+\omega^{\prime\prime}
for some
c\in\mathcal{O}
and some
\omega^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}_{g}H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/F},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)})
.
In particular, if
t\in{\mathrm{Ann}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}}(\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}})
, then

\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}(t)\frac{\omega_{g}}{c}=\frac{t\cdot\omega_{g}}{c}=t\cdot\omega^{\prime}\in H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)}).

As
\omega_{g}
is part of an
\mathcal{O}
-basis of
H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)})
, it follows that
c\mid\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}(t)
.
As
(c_{g})\subset\mathcal{O}
is the ideal generated by the
\phi_{\Gamma_{1}(N)}(t)
,
t\in{\mathrm{Ann}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}}(\mathfrak{p}_{g})
,
it then follows that
c\mid c_{g}
. An analogous argument applies to
c^{\pm}
. This proves part (i).

Part (ii) is an easy consequence of the Gorenstein condition, which holds by Lemma
2.4
.
The module
H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)})
is dual to
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}

as a
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma_{1}(N),\mathcal{O}}
-module. Hence there is an isomorphism of
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
-modules

H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)})_{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}}({\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}},\mathcal{O}_{\lambda})\cong{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}},

the last isomorphism by part (i) of the Gorenstein condition. Then
H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)})_{\mathfrak{m}}[\mathfrak{p}]\cong{\mathrm{Ann}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathfrak{p})
as
{\mathbb{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
-modules, from which the claim that
(c)=(c_{g})
easily follows. A similar argument applies to
c^{\pm}
by also appealing
to property (ii) of the Gorenstein condition.
∎

2.2.10.
Petersson norms and periods

Let
\omega_{g}
denote the holomorphic differential on
X_{0}(N)
that is the unique holomorphic extension of the differential on
Y_{0}(N)
that pulls back to

2\pi ig(z)dz=g(q)\frac{dq}{q}
under the complex uniformisation

\mathfrak{h}/\Gamma_{1}(N)\simeq Y_{1}(N)({\mathbb{C}}),

for
\mathfrak{h}
the upper half plane,
z=x+iy
the complex variable, and
q=e^{2\pi iz}
.
It follows from the
q
-expansion principle that we can take this to be
the differential so denoted in §
2.2.7
:
For the chosen model of
X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}}
and since
p\nmid N
,
\omega_{g}\in H^{0}(X_{1}(N)_{/\mathcal{O}},\Omega_{X_{1}(N)})

if and only if the
q
-expansion coefficients of
w_{N}\cdot g
belong to
\mathcal{O}
, where
w_{N}
is the Atkin–Lehner involution (cf.
[
52
, §1.5.10]
);
since
g
is a newform with trivial Nebentypus, this
latter condition is equivalent to the
q
-expansion of
g
having coefficients in
\mathcal{O}
.

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denote the Petersson inner product on
S_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))_{{\mathbb{C}}}

given by

\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle=\frac{i}{8\pi^{2}}\cdot\int_{X_{1}(N)({\mathbb{C}})}\omega_{g}\wedge\overline{\omega_{g^{\prime}}}=\int_{\mathfrak{h}/\Gamma_{1}(N)}g(z)\overline{g^{\prime}(z)}dxdy.

The congruence period of
g
is defined to be

\Omega_{g}^{cong}=\frac{\langle g,g\rangle}{c_{g}}.

This is related to the optimal periods
\Omega^{\pm}
as follows.

Lemma 2.6
.

(i)

\Omega_{g}^{cong}\sim_{F^{\times}}-i(2\pi)^{-2}\Omega^{+}\Omega^{-}
.

(ii)

If (irr
Q
) holds, then

\Omega_{g}^{cong}\sim_{\mathcal{O}^{\times}}-i(2\pi)^{-2}\cdot\Omega^{+}\Omega^{-}
.

Here and throughout ‘
\sim_{\Box}
’ denotes equality up to multiplication by an element in
\Box
.

This lemma is likely well-known, and essentially proven in
[
145
, Chap. 4, §2]
(see also
[
44
, §4.4]
).
As we have assumed that
g
is a newform with trivial character, the proof is slightly simpler than
in
[
145
]
. The proof from
[
44
]
suffices, but with
\Gamma_{0}(N)
replaced by
\Gamma_{1}(N)

for the purposes of this paper.

Remark 2.7
.

If
g
corresponds to the isogeny class of an elliptic curve
E_{\bullet}
as in Remark
2.3
, then
the conclusion of part (ii) still holds even when (irr
Q
) does not. The modular parameterisation
\pi_{\bullet}:X_{1}(N)\twoheadrightarrow E_{1}\twoheadrightarrow E_{\bullet}

is such that the Néron differential
\omega_{E_{\bullet}}
pulls back to a
p
-adic unit multiple of
\omega_{g}
, and so we have

-i(2\pi)^{-2}\Omega^{+}\Omega^{-}\sim_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}\deg(\pi_{\bullet})^{-1}\langle g,g\rangle.

It remains to note that
\deg(\pi_{\bullet})
equals
c_{g}
(up to a
p
-adic unit), and this follows from a simple modification of the arguments used to prove

[
38
, Lem. 3.1.2]
.

2.2.11.
The ordinary and supersingular cases

In many of our results and arguments we will consider two possible cases. These are the following.

The ordinary case.
This is the case that
a_{g}(p)
is a unit in the ring of integers of
F_{\lambda}
(equivalently,
\iota_{p}(a_{g}(p))
is a
p
-adic unit), in which case we say that
g
is ordinary.
If
g
is ordinary, then there is exactly one root
\alpha_{p}
of the Hecke polynomial
x^{2}-a_{g}(p)x+p
that is a
p
-adic unit, that is, a unit in the ring of integers of
F_{\lambda}
.

In this case there is an exact sequence

0\rightarrow V^{+}\rightarrow V\rightarrow V^{-}\rightarrow 0

of
F_{\lambda}[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}]
-modules with
V^{\pm}
a one-dimensional
F_{\lambda}
-vector space and
both
V^{+}
and
V^{-}(1)
unramified
F_{\lambda}[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}]
-modules. The action of
{\mathrm{Frob}}_{p}
on

V^{-}(1)
is just multiplication by
\alpha_{p}
.

Let
T^{+}=T\cap V^{+}
and
T^{-}=T/T^{+}
. Then

T^{\pm}
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
-module of rank one, and there is also an exact sequence

0\rightarrow T^{+}\rightarrow T\rightarrow T^{-}\rightarrow 0

of
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}]
-modules.

The supersingular case.
This is the case
8
8

8

If
g
is associated with an elliptic curve, then
p\nmid 2N
being a prime of supersingular reduction need not imply
a_{g}(p)=0

(but only when
p=3
). However, we adopt the terminology for convenience.

where
p\nmid N
and
a_{g}(p)=0
. The roots of the Hecke polynomial at
p
are just
\pm\sqrt{p}
. In this case we say that
g
is supersingular.
If
g
is supersingular, then the residual representation
\overline{T}
is irreducible as a
k_{\lambda}[G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}]
-module (cf.
[
48
, Thm. 2.6]
). In particular, (irr
Q
) always holds in this case.

2.2.12.
Convention

To distinguish objects associated with a specific newform
g
(if necessary), we will add a subscript ‘
g
’ in the notation (if not already present).
This convention will also hold for all notation introduced subsequently.

2.3.
Newforms:
L
-values

Let
g\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N)
be a newform as in §
2.2
.

2.3.1.
L
-functions

Let
L(s,g)
be the usual
L
-function associated with
g
. For
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)>\frac{3}{2}
this is just the (absolutely convergent) Dirichlet
series

L(s,g)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{g}(n)n^{-s}.

If
\chi:({\mathbb{Z}}/m{\mathbb{Z}})^{\times}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
is any Dirichlet character modulo
m
, let
L(s,g,\chi)
be the
\chi
-twisted
L
-series

L(s,g,\chi)=\sum_{n=1\atop(n,m)=1}^{\infty}a_{g}(n)\chi(n)n^{-s}.

If
(N,m)=1
, then
L(s,g,\chi)
is the
L
-function of a weight
2
newform of level
\Gamma_{1}(Nm^{2})
and Nebentypus
\chi^{2}
, denoted
g\otimes\chi
. In particular,

L(s,g\otimes\chi)=L(s,g,\chi)
in this case.

2.3.2.
L
-values: algebraicity

Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character and
F_{\chi}
the extension of the Hecke field
F
obtained by adjoining the values of
\chi
.

Theorem 2.8
.

We have

\mathfrak{g}(\overline{\chi})\cdot\frac{L(1,g,\chi)}{\Omega^{{\mathrm{sgn}}(\chi)}}\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{\chi},(\lambda_{\chi})}

for
\lambda_{\chi}
any prime of
F_{\chi}
over
\lambda
.
Here
\overline{\chi}=\tau\circ\chi
is the complex conjugate of
\chi
,
\mathfrak{g}(\cdot)
is the usual Gauss sum, and

{\mathrm{sgn}}(\chi)\in\{\pm\}
is the sign of
\chi(-1)
.

With
\mathcal{O}_{F_{\chi},(\lambda_{\chi})}
replaced by
F_{\chi}
, this is due to Shimura
[
127
]
.
The result stated here is a straight-forward consequence of the definition of the optimal periods
(cf.
[
137
, §1.6]
and
[
146
, §2]
).

2.3.3.
L
-values: non-vanishing

Let
\ell
be a prime (
\ell=p
is allowed). Let
\mathfrak{X}_{{\mathbb{Q}},\ell}^{cyc}
be the set of Dirichlet characters with
\ell
-power conductor and
\ell
-power order.
We recall the following non-vanishing result for the central
L
-values in the vertical family arising from
\mathfrak{X}_{{\mathbb{Q}},\ell}^{cyc}
, which is due to Rohrlich
[
119
]
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let

\ell
be a prime.
Then,

L(1,g,\chi)\neq 0

for all but finitely many
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}_{{\mathbb{Q}},\ell}^{cyc}
.

We also have the following mod
p
non-vanishing result, essentially
due to Stevens
[
135
, Thm. 2.1]
(cf. Vatsal
[
137
, Rem. 1.12]
).

Theorem 2.10
.

Suppose that (irr
Q
) holds. Let
M
be a positive integer. Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of primes not
dividing
pNM
. Then, for a given choice of sign
\varepsilon=\pm

there exists a Dirichlet character
\chi
with
{\mathrm{sign}}\chi=\varepsilon
and such that
(pNMM_{\Sigma},\mathrm{cond^{r}}(\chi))=1
, where
M_{\Sigma}=\prod_{\ell\in\Sigma}\ell
, and

v_{p}\bigg(\mathfrak{g}(\overline{\chi})\cdot\frac{L^{\Sigma}(1,g,\chi)}{\Omega^{{\mathrm{sgn}}(\chi)}}\bigg)=0.

Here
L^{\Sigma}(s,g,\chi)
is the incomplete
L
-series obtained by omitting the Euler factors at the primes
\ell\in\Sigma
,
and
v_{p}
is the normalized valuation on
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
(so
v_{p}(p)=1
).

To deduce Theorem
2.10
from the results in
[
137
]
it is enough to show that the canonical periods (in the sense of
op. cit.
) for the newform
g
are the same (up to units in
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
) as
the canonical periods for the eigenform

g_{\Sigma}=\sum_{n=1\atop(n,M_{\Sigma})=1}^{\infty}a_{g}(n)q^{n}\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(NM_{\Sigma}^{2})).

This identification of periods is essentially
[
47
, Thm. 4.2]
(more precisely, the proof of this theorem).
In the case that
g
is ordinary this identification of periods is also proved in
[
62
, §4.1]
(see also

[
49
, Thm. 3.6.2]
and its proof in §3.8 of
[
49
]
).

2.3.4.
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
-type abelian varieties

For the newform
g
as above, let
A_{g}
be an associated
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
-type abelian variety over
{\mathbb{Q}}
with dimension
[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]
such that

{\mathcal{O}}_{F}\subset{\mathrm{End}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}(A_{g/{\mathbb{Q}}})
and

L(s,A_{g})=\prod_{\sigma:F\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}}L(s,g^{\sigma}).

Here
g^{\sigma}
denotes the
\sigma
-conjugate.

2.4.
Newforms:
p
-adic Hodge theory

Let
g\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
be a newform as in §
2.2
.
Recall that
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
has been identified with a subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
via the embedding
\iota_{p}
. In this way, we view
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}

as acting on
V
and
T
in the following constructions.

2.4.1.
D_{dR}
,
D_{cris}
, and spaces of modular forms

The
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation
V
is de Rham (cf.
[
79
, §9.4]
).
Let
D_{dR}(V(n))=(V(n)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}B_{dR})^{G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}}
be the Dieudonné module of the Tate-twist
V(n)
.
Then
D_{dR}(V(n))
is a two-dimensional
F_{\lambda}
-space with an
exhaustive and decreasing filtration
(D^{i}_{dR}(V(n)))_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
The étale-de Rham comparison isomorphism yields a canonical identification (cf.
[
79
, §11.2]
and
[
52
, §1.7.1]
)

\bigg(H^{1}_{dR}(X_{1}(N)/{\mathbb{Q}})\otimes F)/\mathfrak{p}(H^{1}_{dR}(X_{1}(N)/{\mathbb{Q}})\otimes F)\bigg)\otimes_{F}F_{\lambda}\simeq D_{dR}(V)

(2.3)

respecting filtrations. In particular, the image of
S_{F}\otimes_{F}F_{\lambda}
in the right-hand side is identified with
D^{1}_{dR}(V)
:

S_{F}\otimes_{F}{F_{\lambda}}\simeq D^{1}_{dR}(V).

(2.4)

Since
p\nmid N
, the
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation
V(n)
is also crystalline.
Let
D_{cris}(V(n))=(V(n)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}B_{cris})^{G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}}
be the crystalline Dieudonné module, which in this case is a two-dimensional
F_{\lambda}
-space
equipped with the crystalline Frobenius automorphism
\varphi_{cris}
. There are canonical identifications

D_{dR}(V(n))\simeq D_{cris}(V(n))

(2.5)

of
F_{\lambda}
-spaces. Note that the left-hand side is equipped with an
F_{\lambda}
-filtration, the Hodge filtration, which induces a filtration on
the right-hand side:
0\subsetneq D_{crys}^{1-n}(V(n))\subsetneq D_{crys}^{-n}(V(n))
.

Let
T_{0}=\mathrm{im}\{H^{1}(X_{1}(N),{\mathcal{O}}_{\lambda})\rightarrow V\}
. This is a sublattice of
T
and equal to
T
if (irr
Q
) holds.
Since
X_{1}(N)
is a curve,
p\nmid N
, and
p>2
, the image
of
H^{1}(X_{1}(N)_{/{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X_{1}(N)})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
in
the left-hand side of (
2.3
) is identified via (
2.5
) with the strongly divisible
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
-lattice

D_{\lambda}(T_{0})\subset D_{cris}(V)
that corresponds to
T_{0}
(cf.
[
79
, §14.22]
) and is contained
in the strongly divisible lattice
D_{\lambda}(T)
. In particular,
D_{\lambda}^{1}(T_{0})=D_{\lambda}(T_{0})\cap D^{1}_{cris}(V)
is
identified with
S_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}\subset S_{F}\otimes_{F}F_{\lambda}
and contained in

D_{\lambda}^{1}(T)=D_{\lambda}(T)\cap D^{1}_{cris}(V)
. In particular,

S_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}\subset D^{1}_{\lambda}(T)
, with equality holding if (irr
Q
) holds.

(2.6)

Remark 2.11
.

The Dieudonné module
D_{cris}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(1))\simeq D_{dR}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(1))
can be naturally identified with
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
so that the strongly divisible

{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-lattice in
D_{cris}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(1))

corresponding to the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}(1)\subset{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(1)
is identified with
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
. From this it follows that there are natural
identifications
